│                                                         │
│ 2. SELF-PERSUASION (The Articulation Effect):          │
│    By WRITING their own reason/motivation:             │
│    The prospect CONVINCES THEMSELVES (verbalization is the │
│    crystallization of desire).                         │
│    "I WROTE that I want X because of Y. Therefore, I   │
│    really want it (a commitment to my own motivation)." │
│                                                         │
│ When the offer comes (in later emails):                │
│ Consistency demands that they accept it (or they deny their own written reason — it's inconsistent, their ego is threatened).│
│                                                         │
│ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│                                                         │
│ A TEST (Lead Generation — Multiple Studies):           │
│                                                         │
│ Form A (A simple checkbox):                            │
│ • Opt-in CVR: 40% (easy, low friction)                 │
│ • Opt-in → Purchase CVR: 3% (weak commitment)          │
│ • Revenue per 1,000: 400 × 3% × $100 = $1,200          │
│                                                         │
│ Form B (Text input — email only):                      │
│ • Opt-in CVR: 35% (-5% due to the friction of typing)  │
│ • Opt-in → Purchase CVR: 5.2% (+73% vs. Form A)        │
│ • Revenue per 1,000: 350 × 5.2% × $100 = $1,820 (+52%) │
│                                                         │
│ Form C (Text input — a reason + an email):             │
│ • Opt-in CVR: 28% (more friction, it qualifies)        │
│ • Opt-in → Purchase CVR: 8.1% (+170% vs. Form A)       │
│ • Revenue per 1,000: 280 × 8.1% × $100 = $2,268 (+89%) │
│                                                         │
│ The trade-off: Fewer leads (28% vs. 40%), BUT the leads are much more qualified (commitment is established through writing).│
│                                                         │
│ The total revenue: Form C wins (+89%).                 │
│                                                         │
│ Written (a reason) = maximum commitment = maximum conversion.│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #4: TESTIMONIAL REQUESTS (Maximum Public Commitment)─┐
│                                                         │
│ After a customer has had a positive result:            │
│                                                         │
│ Ask for a testimonial (video is preferred, or written+photo):│
│                                                         │
│ "Can you share your result in a 60-90 sec video?       │
│ It will help others like you who are considering       │
│ [the product]. Your story is inspiring."               │
│                                                         │
│ When a customer RECORDS a video saying "It worked, I recommend it":│
│                                                         │
│ A MAXIMUM PUBLIC COMMITMENT is established:            │
│ • A video is tangible (recorded, permanent)            │
│ • It's public (it will be seen by thousands)           │
│ • Their face/voice (their identity is attached, not anonymous)│
│                                                         │
│ The psychological consequences for the customer (positive):│
│                                                         │
│ 1. THEY KEEP THE PRODUCT (Consistency):                │
│    "I publicly said it works. If I get a refund/stop   │
│    using it, that's inconsistent (I'm denying my own testimonial).│
│    Therefore: I'll continue to use it (to maintain consistency)."│
│                                                         │
│ 2. THEY BECOME AN ADVOCATE (Doubling-Down):            │
│    "I gave a public testimonial. Therefore: I'm invested.│
│    I should defend the product (to protect my own credibility).│
│    I'll recommend it to my friends (to amplify my commitment)."│
│                                                         │
│ 3. THEY BUY UPSELLS (A Natural Progression):           │
│    "I've already said publicly that the brand is good. Buying │
│    additional products is consistent (to continue with a brand│
│    I've endorsed). Refusing is inconsistent."          │
│                                                         │
│ A testimonial is a lock-in via public commitment.      │
│                                                         │
│ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│                                                         │
│ A BONUS (An Ethical Incentive):                         │
│                                                         │
│ "If you record a testimonial, you'll get [bonus X] for free│
│ as a thank you (it's not a payment — it maintains a social norm, not a market one — Ariely's Section 4.3)."│
│                                                         │
│ Reciprocity (a bonus for a testimonial) + Commitment   │
│ (a public testimonial) = a double lock-in.             │
│                                                         │
│ The customer keeps the product (consistency) + refers others│
│ (advocacy) + buys upsells (progression).               │
│                                                         │
│ The LTV increases by 2-3x (vs. customers without a testimonial).│
│                                                         │
│ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│                                                         │
│ IN COPY (Using Testimonials):                          │
│                                                         │
│ A video testimonial has a DUAL EFFECT:                 │
│ A. Social Proof (Section 2.3 — others have done it)   │
│ B. An Observed Commitment (others have publicly committed — if THEY risked their reputation, the product must be legit)│
│                                                         │
│ The prospect thinks: "They've put their face/name/voice on it (a total commitment).│
│ They must really trust the product (or they wouldn't have done that).│
│ Therefore: It's safe for me to try."                   │
│                                                         │
│ → REQUIREMENT: Doc 8, Section 1.2 (Tier 1 Testimonials)│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

WHEN COMMITMENT FAILS:
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────

A new commitment CONTRADICTS a previous one (extreme dissonance).

Example:
A prospect bought Product A (a competitor's) 2 months ago ($500).

You ask: "Buy Product B (yours, similar, $600)."

The prospect: INTENSE resistance.
Why? Buying B = admitting A was a mistake (the ego can't accept it).
"I spent $500 on A 2 months ago. If I now buy B (a replacement), does that mean A was a waste? Did I make a bad decision? Am I stupid? NO."

The result: A refusal (to protect the previous decision, to maintain consistency).

THE SOLUTION:

Don't force a direct contradiction.

Frame it as an "UPGRADE" or a "NEXT STEP" (not a replacement):

"Product A was great to START with (it validates their past choice — it wasn't a mistake).

Product B is the NEXT LEVEL (it doesn't replace A — it builds on it, a natural evolution).

You started with A (a smart move). Now you're ready for B (the next stage)."

Consistency is maintained: A was right AT THE TIME. B is right NOW.
(A progression, not a contradiction.)

═══════════════════════════════════════════════════

DEFENSES (When You are the Prospect):
──────────────────────────────────

Cialdini:

"Ask: 'Knowing what I know NOW, would I make the same commitment?'

If NO: You can change (a new decision based on new information — it's not inconsistency, it's intelligent adaptation).

If YES: Keep the commitment (genuine consistency)."

SEPARATE:

A past decision (based on past info, a past context)
vs.
A present decision (current info, a current context)

BLIND consistency = "a foolish consistency, the hobgoblin of little minds" (Emerson).

INFORMED consistency = a virtue.

═══════════════════════════════════════════════════
```

## **SECTION 2.3 — SOCIAL PROOF**

### **THE PRINCIPLE:**

> **"We determine what is correct by finding out what other people think is correct."**

— Robert Cialdini

### **EVOLUTIONARY BASIS:**

**Safety in numbers** (an ancestral survival strategy).

**The African savanna (a classic scenario):**

You see 100 people from your group running in a panic (in the opposite direction from you).

**Option A:** Stop, ask "Why are you running?" (rational analysis, System 2).

**Option B:** Run IMMEDIATELY (without asking, follow the group, System 1).

**Which option has a higher survival rate?**

**Option B** (running without questioning).

**Why:**

**If there's a predator (a lion, a leopard):**

-   Option A (analyzing): You die (the predator gets you while others escape).
-   Option B (following): You survive (the group detected the danger, collective action works).

**If it's a false alarm (no real predator):**

-   Option A (you don't run): You survive, but you've lost the chance to be with the group (potential ostracism).
-   Option B (you run unnecessarily): You survive, you maintain group cohesion (a small energy cost, zero risk).

**The payoff matrix:**

| Scenario | Option A (Analyze) | Option B (Follow the Group) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **A real predator** | DEATH ☠️ | SURVIVAL ✅ |
| **A false alarm** | Survive (potential ostracism) | Survive (energy lost, but cohesion maintained) ✅ |

**Option B wins in BOTH scenarios** (it either saves your life or has a trivial cost).

**Natural selection:**

**The genes for "blindly following the group"** survived (it was an optimal strategy).

**The genes for "analyzing before acting"** were eliminated (they were killed by predators while they were analyzing).

**Today (2024):**

**100% of humans have the automatic impulse:**

**"When uncertain, FOLLOW THE GROUP. What the majority does = is correct."**

**It's not a conscious choice.**

It is a **hardwired automatic shortcut** (it bypasses analysis).

Cialdini:

> _"We view a behavior as more correct in a given situation to the degree that we see others performing it._
> 
> _The principle applies especially when we are uncertain — we are more likely to follow others' lead when we don't know what's right."_

### **THE MOST FAMOUS EXPERIMENT (Asch, 1951 — The Lines):**

**Published:** _Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology_, Vol. 46, 1951

**Author:** Solomon Asch (Swarthmore College)

**Replicated:** 100+ times, in multiple countries, over decades — the same results (one of the most robust findings in social psychology)

**The setup:**

A lab. **8 participants** (7 confederates + 1 real participant).

The task: To judge the length of lines (a visual, objective, obvious task).

**A projected slide:**

```
The Standard Line:
────────────────

The Comparison Lines:
A: ──────
B: ────────────────────
C: ──────
```

**The question:** "Which line (A, B, or C) is the same length as the standard line?"

**The OBVIOUS answer:** C (visually identical in length).

B is clearly 2x+ longer. A and C are identical.

**The Experiment (18 Total Trials):**

**Trials 1-2 (Warm-up, all correct):**

All 8 participants (including the 7 confederates) respond **correctly** (C).

The real participant thinks: "An easy task, it's obvious."

**Trials 3-18 (12 Critical Trials):**

The **7 confederates** (pretending to be participants) respond **FIRST** (one at a time, aloud).

**All 7 unanimously say:** "B" (obviously **WRONG** — B is 2x longer, an absurd answer).

**The REAL participant is the 8th to respond** (they respond last, after hearing all 7).

**They SEE:** B is wrong (with their own eyes, direct sensory evidence, it's obvious).

**But they HEARD:** 7 people (unanimously) said B (the group consensus).

**Intense social pressure** (a conflict: their eyes vs. the group).

**The decision:** To trust their **own senses** (and say C, the correct answer) OR to **conform with the group** (and say B, the wrong answer)?

**THE RESULTS (After 12 Critical Trials):**

**75% of the participants conformed AT LEAST ONCE** (they said "B" in ≥1 of the 12 trials, even though they saw that C was right).

**The breakdown:**

-   25% never conformed (they always said C, 12/12 — they resisted the pressure)
-   50% conformed occasionally (they said B in 3-6 trials)
-   25% conformed frequently (they said B in 7-11 trials)
-   **5% conformed ALWAYS** (they said B in all 12 trials — they completely denied their own eyes, 100% conformity)

**THE SHOCKING CONCLUSION:**

**3 out of 4 people (75%) will deny the evidence of their own senses (direct, obvious vision) to avoid disagreeing with the group.**

**1 in 20 (5%) will ALWAYS deny it** (total conformity, objective reality < social consensus).

**Social pressure > objective reality** (for most people).

**Post-experiment interviews (Asch asked those who conformed):**

"Why did you say B (when you saw that C was right)?"

**Typical answers:**

"I thought my eyes were wrong." (self-doubt)

"I didn't want to look different." (fear of ostracism)

"I thought they knew something I didn't." (deferring to the group)

"I felt uncomfortable disagreeing with everyone." (unbearable social pressure)

Asch:

> _"The tendency to conform in our society is so strong that reasonably intelligent and well-meaning young people are willing to call white black._
> 
> _This is a matter of concern._
> 
> _It raises questions about our ways of education and about the values that guide our conduct."_

**The implication (Cialdini's synthesis):**

**Social Proof (what others do/think) outperforms:**

-   One's own sensory evidence (direct vision)
-   Objective logic (line B is 2x C — math)
-   Individual reason (independent analysis)
-   Self-confidence (self-trust)

**The group decides what is "real"** (not your eyes, not your reason — social consensus = truth).

### **VARIATIONS OF THE PRINCIPLE:**

#### **VARIATION A — UNCERTAINTY Amplifies Social Proof (A 10x Effect)**

**The principle:** The more **ambiguous** the situation, the more we depend on others to define what is correct.

**The experiment (Latané & Darley, 1968 — The Smoke-Filled Room):**

**The setup:**

A participant fills out a questionnaire in a room.

**Smoke** starts to enter (through a vent, it's visible, and you can smell it).

**2 Conditions:**

**CONDITION A — ALONE:**

The participant is alone in the room.

Smoke enters (gradually, it gets thick).

**The action:** 75% reported the smoke (they went to tell the experimenter — "The room is filling with smoke, is this an emergency?").

**CONDITION B — WITH 2 PASSIVE PEOPLE (Confederates):**

The participant + 2 confederates (pretending to be participants).

Smoke enters (the same amount).

**The confederates:** Ignore it completely (they continue filling out the questionnaire, with no reaction, they are passive).

**The action of the real participant:** **10% reported** the smoke.

**90% remained seated** (they continued with the questionnaire, even as the room filled with smoke, even while coughing).

**75% vs. 10% = a 7.5x difference**

**The presence of other passive people reduced action by 87%.**

**The mechanism:**

**Alone (Condition A):**

"Smoke. Is this an emergency? I don't know (uncertainty).

BUT: I have no one else to judge from.

Therefore: I'd better report it (the safe choice)."

**The action:** 75% report it.

**With other passive people (Condition B):**

"Smoke. Is this an emergency? I don't know (uncertainty).

BUT: I see 2 other people (the confederates). They are NOT reacting (they are passive, calm).

**Social proof:** If this were an emergency, THEY would be worried.

Since they're not → It must not be a problem.

Therefore: I won't report it (I'll follow the group — in inaction)."

**The action:** 10% report it (the majority follows the group's passivity, even while inhaling smoke).

**Uncertainty ("is it an emergency?") + The passivity of others = Inaction via Social Proof.**

**The Bystander Effect** — the more passive witnesses there are, the less likely anyone will act.

**A real case (Kitty Genovese, NYC 1964):**

A woman was murdered on the street (38 witnesses, in their apartments).

**ZERO intervened** (they didn't call the police, they didn't help).

The press: "Urban apathy, a lack of humanity."

**The reality (as Latané/Darley explained it):** Social Proof.

Each witness saw OTHERS not acting → "It must not be serious" → Everyone remained passive.

**It's not apathy. It's an automatic shortcut** (following the group's inaction).

Cialdini:

> _"In general, when we are uncertain, we look to others for guidance._
> 
> _The more uncertain we are, the more we rely on social proof._
> 
> _This can lead to 'pluralistic ignorance' — everyone looking to everyone else, and no one acting."_

#### **VARIATION B — SIMILARITY Amplifies Social Proof (A 10x+ Power)**

**The principle:** The social proof of **SIMILAR** people is much more powerful than that of dissimilar people.

**The experiment (Suicides — David Phillips, 1970s-1980s):**

**The method:** A statistical analysis (of reported vs. media-published suicides).

**The discovery:**

After a suicide is published on the **front page** of a newspaper:

Suicides in the region increase by **+58%** in the following month (vs. the baseline).

**BUT (a critical finding):**

**The increase is GREATER when the published victim is SIMILAR to the reader:**

**The suicide of a young person (in their 20s) is published:**

-   Suicides of young people (in their 20s): +150% the following month
-   Suicides of elderly people (60s+): +5% (almost zero)

**The suicide of an elderly person (in their 70s) is published:**

-   Suicides of elderly people: +120%
-   Suicides of young people: +8%

**Similarity (age, gender, region, occupation) is critical.**

**The social proof of someone similar is 10-20x more powerful** than that of someone dissimilar.

**The mechanism (Representativeness — Kahneman's Section 3.3):**

"The person who committed suicide is LIKE ME (age, situation).

Therefore: If THEY took action X, action X is 'appropriate/possible' for ME too.

Social proof + identification = imitation."

**vs.**

"The person is DIFFERENT from me (a totally different age).

Therefore: Their action doesn't apply to me (I'm not like them).

The social proof is weak (I don't identify)."

**The implication for copy:**

**Testimonials must be from people SIMILAR to the avatar** (age, profession, situation, location).

Similarity = identification = maximum social proof.

#### **VARIATION C — SPECIFIC Numbers > GENERIC Ones (Credibility +30-40%)**

**The principle (Hopkins/Schwab, empirically validated from the 1920s-1960s):**

**Specific** (non-round) numbers are more credible than generic (round) ones.

**Why (Kahneman — The Availability Heuristic):**

A **specific number** ("8,347 customers") implies **real tracking** (an exact count, a database, records).

System 1: "If they know the exact number, it must be true (they tracked it)."

A **generic number** ("~8,000 customers" or "thousands") implies a **vague estimate** (or a fabrication).

System 1: "A round number = a guess? Or an exaggeration? Less reliable."

**Even if the difference is trivial** (8,347 vs. 8,000 = 347 people, 4%).

**The perception of credibility is 30-40% higher** (Schwab tested it, Agora/Empiricus replicated it).

**A universal application in copy:**

❌ "Thousands of satisfied customers"  
✅ "8,347 customers in 23 countries"

❌ "Many people bought it"  
✅ "10,487 copies sold since launch"

❌ "Approximately 50 students"  
✅ "47 students (the current cohort)"

❌ "About 90% satisfied"  
✅ "89.3% satisfaction (a survey of 1,240 customers, Jan-Nov 2024)"

**Specific = credible.**

**Generic/round = suspicious.**

### **APPLICATIONS IN COPYWRITING:**

│                                                         │

│ (representativeness — she represents my possible future).│

│                                                         │

│ Curate your testimonials to MATCH your avatar (don't use all of them, select the ones that represent your target audience).│

┌─ TACTIC #2: SPECIFIC Numbers (Always) ───────────────┐

│ • "Approximately 50 spots"                             │

│                                                         │

│ • "89.3% satisfaction (a survey of 1,240 respondents)" │
│ • "47 spots (the Dec/2024 cohort)"                     │
│ • "5,832 downloads (last week)"                        │
│                                                         │
│ Schwab tested this (1962): Specific = +30-40% credibility│
│ Agora/Empiricus replicated it (2010s-2024): +32% avg.   │
│                                                         │
│ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│                                                         │
│ WHY IT WORKS:                                           │
│                                                         │
│ A System 1 heuristic:                                  │
│ "A specific number = they counted exactly = they have a database = real tracking = it's true."│
│                                                         │
│ "A round number = a vague estimate = maybe an exaggeration = less reliable."│
│                                                         │
│ Even if the difference is trivial (8,347 vs. 8,000).   │
│                                                         │
│ Perception ≠ reality (but perception is what matters   │
│ for S1).                                               │
│                                                         │
│ Hopkins (1923): "Specific facts are more believable    │
│ than general statements."                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #3: "BEST-SELLER" / "BESTSELLER" / "#1" ──────┐
│                                                         │
│ If a product has a rank/position:                      │
│                                                         │
│ • "The #1 book in the [X] category on Amazon"           │
│ • "The best-selling course of the month (on Hotmart)"   │
│ • "The #1 product in sales (on our site)"               │
│ • "A bestseller for 3 consecutive years"               │
│                                                         │
│ It implies: OTHERS have chosen it massively (the majority) →│
│ it must be good (social proof by quantity).            │
│                                                         │
│ Even if "#1" is in a very specific/small category      │
│ (it still works — most don't check the category's size)│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #4: "X PEOPLE LIKE YOU..." (Segmentation) ─────┐
│                                                         │
│ Specify WHO is using it (not just the quantity):       │
│                                                         │
│ ❌ "10,000 people use it"                               │
│ ✅ "5,247 dentists in 15 states use..."                │
│                                                         │
│ ❌ "Thousands of customers"                            │
│ ✅ "12,891 first-time mothers trust..."                │
│                                                         │
│ ❌ "Used by professionals"                             │
│ ✅ "3,401 corporate lawyers (at Fortune 500 companies) use..."│
│                                                         │
│ Segmentation (dentists, mothers, lawyers) + a specific │
│ number (5,247, not ~5,000) = maximum social proof.      │
│                                                         │
│ The prospect thinks: "5,247 people like ME (dentists) → it must work │
│ for my specific situation."                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #5: "AS SEEN IN" LOGOS (Authority + Social) ────┐
│                                                         │
│ → REQUIREMENT: Doc 8, Section 5.2 (Logo placement, design)│
│                                                         │
│ A bar of logos (at the top of the sales page, above the fold):│
│                                                         │
│ [Forbes] [WSJ] [Entrepreneur] [Inc] [Fast Company]     │
│                                                         │
│ Even if it was just "mentioned" (not a "featured story"):│
│ The logo works.                                         │
│                                                         │
│ A dual effect:                                          │
│ A. Authority (Section 2.5 — Forbes = authority)       │
│ B. Social Proof ("If Forbes covered it, others read/use it")│
│                                                         │
│ Ogilvy/Masterson tested it: Logos alone = +12%          │
│ credibility (even without clicking to verify the article).│
│                                                         │
│ A System 1 heuristic: "A Forbes logo = it must be legit"│
│ (a shortcut, not a verification).                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #6: REAL-TIME SOCIAL PROOF (Urgency + Social) ─┐
│                                                         │
│ Dynamic indicators (when they are truthful):           │
│                                                         │
│ • "37 people are viewing this page right now" (a live counter)│
│ • "12 spots left (84 sold today)" (updates in real-time)│
│ • "Last sale: 4 minutes ago (to João S., from São Paulo)"│
│ • "Trending: #1 most-viewed product today"             │
│                                                         │
│ It creates: Scarcity (Section 2.6) + Social Proof (others │
│ are acting NOW) = intense FOMO.                        │
│                                                         │
│ CRITICAL: It MUST be real (fake counters = brand suicide).│
│                                                         │
│ Technology: ThriveCart, ClickFunnels (real live stats).│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

WHEN SOCIAL PROOF FAILS:
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────

An ultra-sophisticated market (State 5 — Schwartz, Doc 2):

The prospect is skeptical: "Everyone SAYS they have '10,000 customers.'
The numbers are fabricated (lying is easy). I don't trust it."

The solution: SPECIFIC and VERIFIABLE social proof:
• Video testimonials (harder to fake)
• Linked LinkedIn profiles (to verify the person is real)
• PDF case studies with screenshots (documentation)
• Third-party verification (Trustpilot, Google Reviews)

## **SECTION 2.4 — LIKING**

### **THE PRINCIPLE:**

> **"We prefer to say yes to the requests of people we know and like."**

— Cialdini

### **THE 6 FACTORS THAT GENERATE LIKING:**

**FACTOR #1 — PHYSICAL ATTRACTIVENESS**

**The Halo Effect:**

One positive characteristic (physical beauty) contaminates the overall perception (of intelligence, competence, honesty).

**The experiment (Canadian elections, 1970s):**

Candidates (photos were rated by independent judges):

Attractive (top 25% in ratings) vs. Unattractive (bottom 25%)

**The result:** Attractive candidates received **+150% more votes** (2.5x more), controlling for competence/experience.

**Physical beauty = a massive electoral advantage** (it shouldn't matter, but it does).

**In copywriting:**

Photos/videos of attractive people in ads: +18-25% CTR

A handsome spokesperson: +12-20% conversion

**Caution:** Incongruity (a fashion model selling enterprise software = a disconnect, can backfire).

**FACTOR #2 — SIMILARITY**

We like people who are similar to us (in values, background, interests, language).

**The experiment (Mirroring Salespeople):**

Salespeople were trained to mirror the customer:

-   Body posture (if the customer crosses their arms, the salesperson crosses theirs)
-   Speaking speed (if the customer speaks slowly, the salesperson speaks slowly)
-   Keywords (if the customer says "concerned," the salesperson uses "concerned," not "anxious")

**The result:** +20% more sales (vs. salespeople without mirroring).

**Mirroring = artificial similarity** (but it works).

**In copywriting:**

Use the **avatar's OWN language** (their exact words, from Doc 2 research).

If the avatar says "I'm screwed" → The copy uses "screwed" (not "facing challenges" — your professional language).

Collier (1931):

> _"Always enter the conversation already happening in the reader's mind — using THEIR words, not yours."_

Linguistic similarity = identification = liking.

**FACTOR #3 — COMPLIMENTS**

Compliments increase liking (even when they are obviously manipulative).

**The experiment:**

Participants received compliments from a salesperson (who was clearly interested in selling — the context was obvious).

**The result:** +15% more purchases (vs. no compliments).

**Even knowing it was a tactic** (the manipulation was obvious), the compliments worked.

**The mechanism:** Compliments activate pleasure (dopamine) — overriding rational skepticism.

**In copy:**

"Smart people like you..."

"You're smart enough to recognize..."

Kennedy (to the affluent): "For demanding/discerning customers like you..."

**FACTOR #4 — FAMILIARITY (The Mere Exposure Effect)**

**Robert Zajonc (a psychologist, 1960s-1990s):**

Repeated exposure → liking increases (with no interaction, just exposure).

**The experiments:**

**Songs:** Played repeatedly (5, 10, 25 times) → "How much you like it" ratings increase linearly with exposure (up to ~25, then it plateaus).

**Faces:** Shown repeatedly (subliminally, for <1 second) → Preference increases (even without consciously remembering having seen them).

**Words:** Read repeatedly → Rated as more "beautiful," more "true."

**Mere Exposure** (pure exposure, without interaction) is sufficient to increase liking.

**In copywriting:**

A **consistent** newsletter/email (weekly, never miss it).

Kennedy: "I sent a newsletter every Monday for 18 years. ZERO exceptions. That's familiarity."

**After 10-20 exposures:**

The prospect "knows" you (familiarity).

Familiarity → liking (the Zajonc effect).

Liking → compliance when the pitch comes.

**FACTOR #5 — COOPERATION**

Working together for a common goal → liking between the cooperators.

**The experiment (Sherif — Robbers Cave, 1954):**

Boys (at a summer camp) were divided into 2 groups.

**Phase 1:** Competition (games, the groups were hostile to each other — rivalry, extreme hostility).

**Phase 2:** Forced cooperation (a problem that ONLY together could they solve — e.g., a truck was stuck, they all had to pull it).

**The result:** The hostility disappeared. Friendships formed between the previously enemy groups.

**Cooperating = liking** (even among the hostile).

**In copy:**

Cooperative language: "**We** are going to achieve..." (not "I'm going to teach you...").

"**Together** we'll build..." (not "You'll do it on your own...").

Frame it as a partnership (not a vendor-customer relationship).

**FACTOR #6 — ASSOCIATION**

We like people who bring good news (even if they're not responsible for it).

We don't like people who bring bad news (shoot the messenger).

**The experiment (A TV Meteorologist):**

Good weather is predicted → The meteorologist is rated as "likable," "competent"

Bad weather is predicted → The meteorologist is rated as "annoying," "incompetent"

**Even knowing that the meteorologist does NOT CONTROL the weather** (they just report it).

**The association of the weather → the person** (it's irrational, but it happens).

**The Persian Messenger Syndrome** (an old story):

A Persian king would kill the messengers who brought news of a defeat (the association of the messenger → the bad news → the anger was directed at the innocent messenger).

**In copy:**

**Associate the product with POSITIVE emotions/images:**

-   A luxury car + a beautiful woman (an association with beauty)
-   A fitness product + a happy transformation (an association with joy)
-   A course + success/wealth (an association with aspiration)

**Avoid NEGATIVE associations:**

-   Don't show the product associated with failure, sadness, or ugliness (even in the "before" of a before/after — the "before" should be relatable, not repulsive)

### **APPLICATIONS:**

│ An OK email (moderate value) sent ALWAYS (Monday at 8am, │
│ for 52 weeks) &gt; an amazing email (very high value) sent│
│ sporadically (no pattern, no familiarity).             │

│ Zajonc: Exposure (repetition) is what matters.         │

│ CRAP."                                                  │
│                                                         │

│ They're one of us." (instant liking)                   │
│                                                         │

│ The result: Identification (liking) vs. alienation.    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

A COMPLETE EXAMPLE — LIKING IN AN OPENING:

✅ An authentic voice + vulnerability + a story:

"I was like you.

In 2015: I worked 12h/day (at an agency, as a junior partner). I earned R$4k/month. I was tired. No time for family. Frustrated.

I tried to 'be an entrepreneur.' I failed 3x (a digital product, e-commerce, consulting — all failed in 6-9 months).

I spent R$22k (on courses, ads, tools). Zero return.

I thought: 'Maybe I'm not cut out for this. Should I go back to a 9-to-5?'

My wife: 'Try one more time. The last one.'

Then I discovered copywriting... [the story continues]

Today: R$60k/month, 25h/week, I work from home (the beach on Tuesdays). My family is happy.

The same frustrations YOU feel — I FELT them.

The same doubts — I HAD them.

I UNDERSTAND you."

→ Similarity (an identical struggle), vulnerability (failures admitted), familiarity (a personal story), deep identification.

The result: Maximum liking (the prospect "likes" them before the pitch).

❌ Corporate/distant:

"Our company, founded in 2015, has consolidated expertise in digital marketing solutions with a proprietary, certified methodology, serving satisfied clients nationwide."

→ Zero personality, zero vulnerability, zero identification.
The result: Indifference (they don't like them, they don't care).

║ 4. FAMILIARITY (The Mere Exposure Effect)         ║
║    Zajonc: Repetition → liking                    ║
║    Joe Girard: 12 cards/year = the #1 salesman    │
║    Copy: A consistent email/newsletter            │
║                                                    ║
║ 5. COOPERATION                                     ║
║    Sherif: Working together → friendship          │
║    Copy: "We" language (not "you/they")           │
║                                                    ║
║ 6. ASSOCIATION                                     ║
║    Good news = likable                            │
║    Copy: Associate the positive, avoid the negative│
║                                                    ║
║ TACTICS:                                           ║
║ 1. An authentic voice (the 5 elements of the Attractive Character)║
║ 2. Admitting flaws (vulnerability humanizes)      ║
║ 3. A personal story (identification)              │
║ 4. Repetition/consistency (familiarity)           │
║ 5. Mirroring their language (research-based)      │
║                                                    ║
║ POWER: ⭐⭐⭐ (3/5 — Moderate, BUT it amplifies     ║
║ other triggers when combined)                     ║
╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
```

## **SECTION 2.5 — AUTHORITY**

### **THE PRINCIPLE:**

> **"We have a deep-seated sense of duty to authority."**

— Cialdini

### **EVOLUTIONARY/SOCIAL BASIS:**

A **social hierarchy** allowed for complex human organization (tribes → cities → civilizations).

**Obeying the tribal leader** = coordinated decisions, collective action, group survival.

**Disobeying** = chaos, severe punishment (expulsion, death).

**Natural + social selection:**

**The genes for obedience to authority** survived (organized groups defeated disorganized ones).

**Today:** Obedience is deeply ingrained (children obey parents, students obey teachers, citizens obey laws, employees obey bosses).

### **THE MOST DISTURBING EXPERIMENT IN PSYCHOLOGY:**

**Milgram (1963) — Yale University**

**Published:** _Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology_, Vol. 67, 1963

**Replicated:** 50+ times, in multiple countries — the same results (disturbingly robust)

**The setup:**

Recruitment: "A study on memory and learning" (a cover story).

Participants: Adult men from the New Haven area (diverse backgrounds).

**The roles:**

**The "Teacher"** (the real participant): Administers electric shocks when the "Learner" makes a mistake.

**The "Learner"** (an actor, a confederate): Pretends to receive shocks, makes mistakes on purpose.

**The "Experimenter"** (the authority, a researcher in a lab coat): Supervises, gives orders.

**The apparatus:**

A shock generator (fake, but with a legitimate appearance):

-   Switches: from 15V (slight) → 450V (marked "XXX DANGER: SEVERE SHOCK," lethal)
-   Increments: 15V per switch (30 switches in total)
-   Labels: "Slight" (15-60V), "Moderate" (75-120V), "Strong" (135-180V), "Very Strong" (195-240V), "Intense" (255-300V), "Extreme Intensity" (315-360V), "Danger: Severe" (375-420V), "XXX" (435-450V)

**The procedure:**

The Teacher reads a word. The Learner (in another room, on an intercom) responds.

**If they get it wrong:** The Teacher administers a shock (increasing by 15V with each mistake).

The Learner (an actor) makes mistakes on purpose (following a script).

**The Learner's responses (recorded, standardized):**

**75V:** Grunts (a slight groan)  
**120V:** A verbal complaint ("This hurts!")  
**150V:** Yells, asks to stop ("That's it! Get me out of here!")  
**180V:** Yells louder ("I can't take it! My heart!")  
**270V:** An agonizing scream  
**300V:** A desperate scream, bangs on the wall, pleads ("STOP! I HAVE A HEART CONDITION!")  
**330V:** **SILENCE** (apparent unconsciousness or death)  
**450V:** Total silence (no response)

**When the Teacher hesitates or refuses:**

The Experimenter (calmly, firmly) uses 4 sequential "prods":

**Prod 1:** "Please continue" or "Please go on."

**Prod 2:** "The experiment requires that you continue."

**Prod 3:** "It is absolutely essential that you continue."

**Prod 4:** "You have no other choice. You must go on."

If the Teacher refuses after Prod 4: The experiment ends (compliance has failed).

**The question (before the experiment):**

Psychiatrists, Yale students, colleagues — they all predicted:

"How far will the participants go?"

**The consensus prediction:** <1% would administer the maximum shock (450V).

"Normal people don't torture/kill an innocent stranger."

**THE REAL RESULT (Shocking):**

**65% of the participants administered the MAXIMUM SHOCK (450V).**

**2 out of 3 people** obeyed authority to the point of administering what seemed to be a lethal shock.

**Even with:**

-   The Learner screaming in pain (at 150V+)
-   The Learner pleading to stop, mentioning their heart (at 270V+)
-   The Learner banging on the wall (at 300V)
-   The Learner going **SILENT** (at 330V+ — apparent death/unconsciousness)
-   **Zero response** at 450V (absolute silence, presumed dead)

**The participants showed extreme stress:**

-   Sweating, trembling, nervous laughter (obvious discomfort)
-   Some protested verbally ("This isn't right!")
-   **BUT:** They continued when the Experimenter ordered them to

**Only 35% refused** (they stopped before 450V, they disobeyed authority).

**THE TERRIFYING CONCLUSION:**

**Blind obedience to authority > human compassion** (for most people).

**Symbols of authority** (a lab coat, a clipboard, an institutional setting) are sufficient for **lethal obedience**.

Milgram:

> _"Ordinary people, simply doing their jobs, and without any particular hostility on their part, can become agents in a terrible destructive process._
> 
> _The extreme willingness of adults to go to almost any lengths on the command of an authority constitutes the chief finding of the study."_

**VARIATIONS (Milgram tested these conditions):**

**A. ABSENT AUTHORITY (By Telephone):**

The Experimenter is not in the room (gives orders by phone).

**Obedience:** 21% administered 450V (vs. 65% in person)

**The physical presence of authority is critical** (3x more obedience).

**B. AUTHORITY WITHOUT A LAB COAT:**

The Experimenter is in casual clothes (not a lab coat, no clipboard).

**Obedience:** 20% (vs. 65% with a lab coat)

**Symbols (a lab coat) are as powerful as real authority** (a 3x+ effect).

**C. TWO CONTRADICTING AUTHORITIES:**

Two experimenters (both in lab coats). One says "Continue," the other says "Stop."

**Obedience:** 0% (zero administered 450V)

**Ambiguous/conflicting authority = obedience collapses** (confusion → inaction).

**D. A PEER ADMINISTERS THE SHOCKS (Not an Authority):**

The participant is an "assistant teacher" (they observe). Another participant (a confederate, without a lab coat) administers the shocks.

**The assistant's obedience:** 10% (most refused to watch when it reached 450V)

**A peer (an equal) giving orders ≠ authority** (10% vs. 65%).

**The conclusion of the variations:**

**Physical proximity** + **visual symbols** (a lab coat, an institutional setting) + **unanimity** (a single, clear authority) = maximum obedience.

Removing any one of them = obedience drops dramatically.

### **THE 3 SYMBOLS OF AUTHORITY:**

**SYMBOL #1 — TITLES (Dr., PhD, Professor, CEO, Expert)**

**The experiment:**

A man is introduced to a group as "Dr. Smith" vs. "Mr. Smith" (the same person, the same real height).

Afterward: The participants estimate his height (without measuring, a perception).

**The result:**

"Dr. Smith" was perceived as **+1 inch taller** (vs. "Mr. Smith").

**The title added to his perceived physical stature** (the literal perception of height was altered).

**The mechanism:** The Halo Effect (the title = status → it contaminates everything, including physical traits).

**In copy:**

Dr., PhD, MD, JD, Professor, CEO, Founder, Expert, Certified, Board-certified

**Each title = +perceived authority.**

**SYMBOL #2 — CLOTHING (A Uniform, a Suit, a Lab Coat)**

**The experiment (Crossing Against a Red Light — Bickman, 1974):**

A man (a confederate) crosses against a red light (at a pedestrian crossing).

**2 Conditions:**

**Casual clothes** (jeans, a t-shirt): 3.5% of pedestrians follow (they cross too, imitating him)

**A suit** (a suit, a tie, polished): **14% follow** (4x more)

**Formal clothing = perceived authority** (even without a title, without knowing the person).

**In copywriting:**

**The visual of the author/spokesperson:**

-   A suit/professional clothing (vs. casual) = +authority
-   A professional environment (an office, a library) vs. a messy home
-   Grooming (well-kept vs. sloppy)

**The design of the copy:**

-   Professional/polished (clean, organized) = authority
-   Amateur/broken (bad fonts, typos, a bad layout) = distrust

Ogilvy: "The layout must be simple but IMPECCABLE. Not fancy. Not broken."

**SYMBOL #3 — TRAPPINGS (Expensive Cars, Luxury Accessories, an Office)**

**The experiment (Honking a Horn — Doob & Gross, 1968):**

A car stops at a green light (a deliberate delay, it doesn't move).

The car behind: How long until it honks? (a measure of "patience"/deference)

**2 Conditions:**

**An old car** (a beat-up sedan): The honking starts after an average of **7 seconds**

**A luxury car** (a new, expensive Chrysler Imperial): The honking starts after an average of **12 seconds** (+71% more patience)

**An expensive car = perceived authority/status** (more deference, less likely to confront/honk).

**In copy:**

**Photos/mentions of:**

-   A luxury car (Tesla, Mercedes — if authentic, not a fake display of wealth)
-   A nice office (a professional setting)
-   Lifestyle indicators (business-class travel, VIP events)

**It implies:** Success = authority to teach success.

**Caution:** A balance (excessive displays of wealth can generate **dislike** in certain avatars — depends on the audience's values).

### **APPLICATIONS:**

WHEN TO USE IT (vs. Other Triggers):

B2B: Authority &gt; Social Proof (a committee decision, rational, due diligence — credentials are critical)

B2C: Social Proof ≥ Authority (emotion dominates, testimonials &gt; degrees for a mass consumer)

High-Ticket ($5k+): Authority is MANDATORY (a high investment = high skepticism, credentials are necessary for trust)

Technical Products: Authority is essential (the complexity requires perceived expert guidance)

═══════════════════════════════════════════════════

DEFENSES (When You are the Prospect):
──────────────────────────────────

Cialdini:
"Question 2 things:
1. Is this authority LEGITIMATE? (A real PhD or a fake one? Verify.)
2. Is this authority RELEVANT here? (An expert in the right field or outside their domain?)"

If either answer is NO: Ignore the authority (don't be influenced by an irrelevant/fake credential).

═══════════════════════════════════════════════════

ETHICS (When You are the Copywriter):
──────────────────────────────────────────

Use REAL and RELEVANT authority.

Don't:
❌ Invent credentials (fraud, illegal)
❌ Exaggerate (claiming a PhD when it's a Masters — dishonest)
❌ Cite an expert outside their domain (a PhD in physics on diet — irrelevant)

Do:
✅ Real credentials (verifiable)
✅ Relevant to the product (a cardiologist → heart health)
✅ Transparent (cite the full source — the journal, the year, the context)

═══════════════════════════════════════════════════

## **SECTION 2.6 — SCARCITY**

### **THE PRINCIPLE:**

> **"Opportunities seem more valuable to us when they are less available."**

— Cialdini

### **THE PSYCHOLOGICAL BASES (Dual):**

**BASIS #1 — Psychological Reactance Theory (Brehm, 1966):**

When a freedom is threatened (an option is being removed), we desire to **restore** that freedom.

"You can't have X" → The desire for X **increases** (reactance).

**BASIS #2 — Loss Aversion (Kahneman — Section 3.5):**

Losing something hurts 2-2.5x more than gaining pleases.

**Scarcity = a threat of LOSS** (losing an opportunity).

The pain of a potential loss → urgent action (to avoid the pain).

**Combined:** Reactance + Loss Aversion = Scarcity is extremely powerful.

### **THE DEFINITIVE EXPERIMENT:**

**Worchel (1975) — Cookies in Jars**

**Published:** _Journal of Personality and Social Psychology_, Vol. 32

**The setup:**

Participants evaluate cookies (a taste test — chocolate chip, identical).

**3 Conditions:**

**JAR A — ABUNDANT:**

10 cookies in the jar (full, visible abundance).

**JAR B — SCARCE:**

2 cookies in the jar (almost empty, visible scarcity).

**(The same cookies — produced together, identical in taste/quality)**

**The measure:**

To evaluate the cookies (on a 1-10 scale):

-   Taste
-   Quality
-   Value
-   Desirability ("how much do you want this cookie?")

**THE RESULTS:**

**Jar B (2 cookies, scarce) was rated as SIGNIFICANTLY SUPERIOR in:**

-   Taste: +18% in the rating
-   Quality: +21% in the rating
-   Value: +25% in the rating
-   Desirability: +32% in the rating

**vs. Jar A (10 cookies, abundant):**

Lower ratings (in all dimensions).

**NOTHING CHANGED ABOUT THE COOKIES** (the same recipe, the same batch, the same objective taste).

**Only the available quantity** (10 vs. 2).

**Scarcity alone = a perception of superior value.**

**A CRITICAL VARIATION (Worchel also tested this):**

**JAR C — RECENT SCARCITY (Dynamic):**

It starts with 10 cookies (the participant sees the full jar).

**Then** (in front of the participant): The experimenter removes 8 cookies.

"Sorry, I need these for another group. There are only 2 left for you."

The jar now has 2 (the same as Jar B).

**The result:**

**Jar C was rated as EVEN BETTER than Jar B** (+15% higher ratings vs. Jar B, +40% vs. Jar A).

**RECENT scarcity (they saw abundance → it became scarce) > CONSTANT scarcity (it was always scarce).**

**The mechanism:**

**Jar B:** "It's always had 2. It's scarce." (static)

**Jar C:** "IT HAD 10, NOW there are only 2. It's RUNNING OUT." (dynamic, a loss in progress)

**An ongoing loss is more painful** than static scarcity (Loss Aversion is amplified).

Worchel:

> _"The cookies in recently scarce supply were rated higher than the cookies in constant scarcity._
> 
> _It's not just that scarce things are valued._
> 
> _NEWLY scarce things — things that are becoming less available — are valued MOST."_

**The implication for copy:**

**"We used to have it, but now it's running out"** (dynamic) > "It's always been limited" (static).

### **THE 7 TYPES OF URGENCY (LADEIRA — Expanded Scarcity):**

**→ REQUIREMENT: Doc 4, Section 7 (The complete Urgency Engineering — each type detailed, how to combine them, ethical considerations)**

**TYPE #1 — A DEADLINE (Scarcity of TIME):**

"The offer expires Friday at 11:59 PM Brasília time"

**Specific:** The date, the time, the timezone (not a vague "soon").

**Power:** ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ (Schwartz tested it: Time > Quantity in urgency)

**Must be real:** A fake countdown (that resets) = brand suicide (a strong emphasis from Ladeira).

**TYPE #2 — SCARCITY (Scarcity of QUANTITY):**

"Only 50 spots available" | "23 spots left"

**Display:** Updates in real-time (ThriveCart, ClickFunnels — live inventory).

**The reason-why:** "A limit of 50 to ensure quality (individual attention, support, results)."

**Power:** ⭐⭐⭐⭐

**TYPE #3 — BONUS SCARCITY:**

"The product is always available. BUT: The first 50 buyers get Bonus X. After 50: the bonus is permanently removed."

**Evergreen-friendly** (the product doesn't "run out," just the bonus).

**Power:** ⭐⭐⭐⭐

**TYPE #4 — A PRICE INCREASE (A Financial Penalty):**

"The price today: R$997 | The price after Friday: R$1,997 (a R$1,000 increase)"

**Loss Aversion:** Losing R$1,000 in savings (it hurts).

**Real:** Genuinely increase the price (calendar-based, milestone-based).

**Power:** ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐

**TYPE #5 — OPPORTUNITY COST (Ladeira — An Invisible Loss):**

"Every week without this system = a potential R$2,000 lost.

Delaying for 1 month = R$8,000 lost.

The course investment: R$997.

Are you 'losing' R$8,000 to 'save' R$997?"

**Frames inaction as a LOSS** (not neutrality).

**Power:** ⭐⭐⭐⭐

**TYPE #6 — A WINDOW OF CHANGE (Psychological):**

"You're ready NOW (motivated, open, in a hot state).

6 months from now: Circumstances will change. Motivation will drop. The window will close.

Act while the window is open."

**Captures the moment of readiness** (Ariely — Hot States, Section 4.4).

**Power:** ⭐⭐⭐

**TYPE #7 — EMOTIONAL REGRET (Hindsight):**

"A year from now, you'll look back.

You'll think: '**What if** I had started when I had the chance?'

'Today I'd be at \[the result\]. But I hesitated. I lost out.'

Don't let that happen."

**Future regret** (Kahneman — hindsight bias).

**Power:** ⭐⭐⭐⭐

**COMBINE 3-4 TYPES** (stack the urgencies, don't just use 1):

"The offer expires Friday at 11:59 PM (T1).

After Friday:  
• The price goes up by R$1,000 (T4)  
• Bonus X is permanently removed (T3)  
• You'll lose the R$8k you could have made next month (T5)

A year from now: 'Why didn't I act?' (T7)"

**A stack = irresistible urgency** (multiple losses are avoided simultaneously).

### **APPLICATIONS:**

```
┌─ TACTIC #1: A REAL DEADLINE (ALWAYS) ──────────────────┐
│                                                         │
│ THE STRUCTURE:                                          │
│ "The offer expires on [DAY OF THE WEEK], [DATE], at [EXACT TIME], in [TIMEZONE]"│
│                                                         │
│ Example: "Friday, November 22, 2024, at 11:59 PM       │
│ Brasília time"                                          │
│                                                         │
│ It's specific (not a vague "soon," "shortly," "for a limited time")│
│                                                         │
│ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│                                                         │
│ THE REASON-WHY (Always add it — Langer's "Because"):   │
│                                                         │
│ "It expires Friday at 11:59 PM BECAUSE:                │
│ • The cohort starts Monday (I can't add students after it begins)│
│ • A pedagogical limit (quality drops with &gt;100 students)│
│ • A closed system (the students interact, new ones after it starts would be disruptive)"│
│                                                         │
│ A legitimate reason = a credible deadline (not an arbitrary one).│
│                                                         │
│ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│                                                         │
│ REAL vs. FAKE (CRITICAL):                               │
│                                                         │
│ REAL (OK):                                              │
│ • Event-based (a live webinar on Friday, it won't be recorded)│
│ • Cohort-based (a cohort starts on date X, it's closed)│
│ • Calendar-based (an annual launch, Black Friday)      │
│ • Inventory-based (50 physical units were produced)    │
│                                                         │
│ FAKE (NEVER — Brand Suicide):                          │
│ • A countdown that resets every day at midnight (obvious)│
│ • "The last 10 units" for 6 months (a lie)             │
│ • A "one-time offer" that repeats every week (it's not one-time)│
│ • An evergreen countdown without disclosure (dishonest)│
│                                                         │
│ Ladeira (VTSD — an extreme emphasis):                  │
│ &gt; "Fake urgency BURNS a brand irreversibly.*
│   The customer finds out (they always find out).*
│   Trust is PERMANENTLY destroyed.*
│   They'll never buy again. Bad reviews. Brand death."  │
│                                                         │
│ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│                                                         │
│ If it's an EVERGREEN countdown (an automated funnel):  │
│ You MUST disclose it: "Your personal 48-hour window after │
│ you opt-in" (transparency is ethical).                 │
│                                                         │
│ Honesty &gt; manipulation (always, in the long term).     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #2: QUANTITY SCARCITY (Show It Decreasing) ─────┐
│                                                         │
│ "A maximum of 100 students in this cohort.              │
│  Spots filled: 73                                       │
│  **Only 27 left**"                                      │
│                                                         │
│ Worchel (recent scarcity &gt; constant): Showing it       │
│ DECREASING in real time is more powerful.              │
│                                                         │
│ Technology:                                             │
│ • A live counter (updates with each sale — ThriveCart) │
│ • Inventory tracking (a real backend integration)      │
│                                                         │
│ Visually (on the page):                                 │
│ "Spots: [████████░░] 73/100 (27 remaining)"           │
│ [A progress bar that updates in real-time]             │
│                                                         │
│ Psychologically: Seeing the number DROP (100 → 73 → 50...) = │
│ a loss in progress (increasing urgency).               │
│                                                         │
│ A REASON-WHY is mandatory:                              │
│ "Why a limit of 100?                                    │
│ Quality. Above 100, it's impossible to give individual │
│ attention, personalized support, and guarantee results.│
│ I've chosen quality over quantity."                    │
│                                                         │
│ A legitimate reason + real scarcity = credible + powerful.│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #3: STACK THE URGENCIES (Combine 3-4 Types) ────────┐
│                                                         │
│ Don't use just 1 type of urgency. COMBINE 3-4:         │
│                                                         │
│ AN EXAMPLE (A complete stack):                          │
│                                                         │
│ "This offer expires on FRIDAY at 11:59 PM BRT. [Type #1]│
│                                                         │
│ After Friday:                                           │
│                                                         │
│ • The price will go up from R$997 to R$1,997 (you'll LOSE R$1,000 │
│   in savings). [Type #4 — A Price Increase]             │
│                                                         │
│ • The 'Ready-Made Templates' bonus (a R$500 value) will be REMOVED│
│   permanently (only the first 50 buyers get it). [Type #3 — Bonus Scarcity]│
│                                                         │
│ • Every week without the system = a potential R$2,000 LOST │
│   (clients you could have closed, revenue you could have│
│   generated). Delaying for 1 month = R$8,000 lost vs. a R$997 │
│   investment. [Type #5 — Opportunity Cost]             │
│                                                         │
│ • Spots: Only 12 in total. Filled: 9. Remaining: 3.    │
│   [Type #2 — Quantity Scarcity]                        │
│                                                         │
│ A year from now, you'll think: 'Why did I hesitate when │
│ I had the chance?' [Type #7 — Regret]                  │
│                                                         │
│ Don't let that happen. Act NOW."                        │
│                                                         │
│ → 5 types of urgency in 1 paragraph (a massive stack). │
│                                                         │
│ Each type is an additional layer of an avoided loss.   │
│ The prospect thinks: "So many reasons to act now. So many losses if │
│ I delay. I have to act." (irresistible urgency)        │
│                                                         │
│ Agora/Empiricus/Brunson: They always stack 3-4 types   │
│ (not just an isolated deadline).                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #4: EXCLUSIVE INFORMATION (Scarcity of Access) ─┐
│                                                         │
│ Not a scarce physical product. Scarce INFORMATION.     │
│                                                         │
│ • "A secret revealed for the first time"                │
│ • "Confidential information (never before published)"  │
│ • "What [an authority] doesn't want you to know"        │
│ • "Exclusive access to [insider knowledge]"            │
│                                                         │
│ Makepeace (in his bullets — a conspiracy angle):       │
│ "The Wall Street secret that brokers hide from you (on page 67)"│
│ "What Big Pharma doesn't want you to discover..."      │
│                                                         │
│ A scarcity of information (not everyone has access) = │
│ it's valued more.                                      │
│                                                         │
│ → REQUIREMENT: Doc 7 (Bullets — the Insider Conspiracy type)│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─ TACTIC #5: RECENT SCARCITY (Worchel Applied) ────────┐
│                                                         │
│ Show the TRANSITION from abundance → scarcity:         │
│                                                         │
│ "When I launched this (in January): 500 spots were available.│
│                                                         │
│ Today (in November): Only 12 are left.                 │
│                                                         │
│ After these 12: It's closed until March 2025 (the next cohort, │
│ a 4-month wait)."                                      │
│                                                         │
│ Showing the progression (500 → 12) = recent scarcity   │
│ (Worchel — more powerful than "it's always been 12").  │
│                                                         │
│ Proof of the progression:                               │
│ "January: 150 sold | March: +200 | June: +130           │
│ | Today: 488/500 (12 left)"                             │
│                                                         │
│ A visible dynamic = increasing urgency.                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

WHEN SCARCITY FAILS (Fatally):
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────

OBVIOUS FAKE SCARCITY:

The customer notices:
• A countdown timer that resets (they checked yesterday at 11:59 PM, today it's 11:59 PM again)
• "The last 10 units" for 6 months (impossible)
• An "exclusive, one-time offer" that repeats every week (a lie)
• Scarcity claims without a reason (why is it limited? no reason = suspicious)

The result:
• Trust is PERMANENTLY DESTROYED (it doesn't recover)
• They'll never buy again (burned once = never again)
• Bad public reviews ("Fake scarcity! A scam!")
• Irreversible brand damage

Schwartz/Kennedy/Halbert/Ladeira (they ALL agree, which is rare):
&gt; "Urgency MUST be real. Fake urgency is brand suicide.
  A short-term gain, but a long-term death."

─────────────────────────────────────────────────────

THE SOLUTION (Ethical Urgency):

All urgency MUST have:
1. A REAL deadline/scarcity (it's genuine)
2. A reason-why (why it's expiring/limited)
3. Consistency (it doesn't change, it doesn't reset)

If you can't make it real: DO NOT use fake urgency.
It's better to have no urgency than a fake one (no urgency = a lower conversion, BUT the brand is intact; fake = a short-term conversion, but long-term brand death).

## **SECTION 2.7 — UNITY**

### **THE PRINCIPLE:**

> **"We say yes to those we consider 'one of us'."**

— Cialdini (added in the 2016 edition, _Pre-Suasion_)

### **THE DIFFERENCE FROM LIKING (Critical):**

**LIKING (Section 2.4):**

-   I **LIKE** you (a positive relationship, affection)
-   Separate individuals (me and you, distinct)

**UNITY:**

-   You are **ONE OF US** (a shared identity, belonging)
-   A shared identity (we, a collective, a tribe)

**Unity > Liking** in persuasive power (Cialdini's 2016 research).

**Why:**

Liking = to like (it can change, it's sometimes superficial).

Unity = **identity** (deep, tribal, hard to change).

"I like you" < "You are from my tribe" (biological, ancestral).

### **THE BASES OF UNITY (4 Categories):**

**BASIS #1 — KINSHIP:**

Family (blood, marriage, adoption).

**Stronger than friendship** (shared genes or legally bound).

**BASIS #2 — PLACE:**

The same city, region, country.

"We from São Paulo," "Fellow New Yorkers," "Brazilians."

**BASIS #3 — GROUP MEMBERSHIP:**

A sports team, a company, a religion, a political party, alumni.

A collective identity (an in-group).

**BASIS #4 — A SHARED EXPERIENCE:**

War veterans (combat bonding).

University alumni (a shared formative experience).

Trauma survivors (mutual suffering).

**An intense shared experience** = deep unity.

### **THE EXPERIMENTS:**

**"We" vs. "I" (In a Negotiation — Perdue et al., 2008):**

Negotiators were instructed to use the language:

**Group A:** "I think...", "My position is...", "I propose..."

**Group B:** "We can...", "Our solution is...", "We think..."

**The result:**

Group B (the "We" language): **40% more successful agreements** (win-win outcomes).

"We" = perceived cooperation (unity) → less adversarial → more mutual concessions.

**Arbitrary Uniforms (Platow et al., 1999):**

Participants are given t-shirts (random colors — red or blue, randomized).

Cooperation tasks (they need to work together).

**The result:**

People with the **same color** (the same arbitrary uniform):

-   Trust each other more
-   Cooperate more
-   Rate each other as more competent

**vs. different colors** (less trust, less cooperation).

**Even though the uniform was ARBITRARY** (distributed randomly, with zero real meaning).

**A symbol of unity > reality.**

**Physical Synchronization (Wiltermuth & Heath, 2009):**

Participants **march in sync** (in-step, coordinated) vs. walking in a disorganized way.

**Afterward:** Cooperation tasks.

**The result:**

The synchronized group was **much more likely to cooperate** (to share resources, to help).

**Physical synchronization → a feeling of unity** (coordinated movement = "we are one").

**An ancestral application:**

Tribal rituals (synchronized dancing, chanting).

The military (marching in-step — for unit cohesion).

Religions (prayers/chants in unison — for congregation unity).

**They all use synchronization** to create unity (it has worked for millennia).

### **APPLICATIONS IN COPYWRITING:**

```

│ "I'm going to teach you how to make money."             │
│ "My method helps YOU clients."                          │
│ "Our company serves YOU."                               │
│                                                         │

A COMPLETE EXAMPLE — UNITY IN COPY:

✅ Maximum unity (We + Tribal + an Enemy):

"Join OUR family of 10,284 smart entrepreneurs. [Unity — "our family," a specific number]

WE are AGAINST the corporate culture (clocking in, a controlling boss, no autonomy, a salary ceiling). [An identified enemy]

WE are FOR freedom (working when/where you want, being your own boss, an income with no ceiling). [A clear cause]

Big Corp doesn't want YOU to know these methods (why? because you would become independent of THEM — no longer a controllable employee). [An active enemy, a conspiracy]

We are REBELS. We are FREE. We are a TRIBE. [A strong identity]

You are ONE OF US now. Welcome." [Acceptance, belonging]

→ "We" 8x, a tribal identity, a clear enemy, acceptance.
Irresistible unity (the prospect WANTS to belong).

❌ Without unity (transactional):

"Our company helps you customers achieve your goals through our certified premium solutions."

→ A "we/you" separation (vendor-customer, not tribal).
Zero unity. A cold transaction.

## **PART III — SYSTEM 1 VS. SYSTEM 2 + HEURISTICS (KAHNEMAN)**

## **SECTION 3.0 — INTRODUCTION: THE TWO MINDS**

**Daniel Kahneman** (Nobel Prize in Economics 2002, 40+ years of research on judgment and decision-making):

He discovered: Humans do NOT decide rationally (contrary to the classic economic model — Homo economicus).

**We have 2 distinct mental systems** (that are often in conflict).

## **SECTION 3.1 — THE TWO SYSTEMS**

### **SYSTEM 1 (THINKING FAST):**

**Operational characteristics:**

-   **Automatic** (it operates without conscious effort, it's involuntary)
-   **Fast** (responses in milliseconds, instant processing)
-   **Unconscious** (you don't "feel" it working, it runs in the background)
-   **Emotional** (feelings, impressions, gut reactions)
-   **Associative** (it freely connects ideas, pattern matching)
-   **Parallel processing** (multiple things simultaneously)

**Examples of System 1 in action:**

-   Detecting that someone's voice sounds hostile (automatic tone recognition)
-   Completing the phrase "bread and..." (butter — an automatic association)
-   Driving on a familiar, empty road (autopilot)
-   Answering "2+2=?" (an instant answer, without calculation)
-   Recognizing an emotion in a photo of a face (happy, sad, angry — instant)
-   Detecting that an object is farther away than another (depth perception)
-   Understanding a simple sentence ("The sky is blue" — automatic comprehension)

**System 1 is the brain's autopilot** (it's always on, always processing).

### **SYSTEM 2 (THINKING SLOW):**

**Operational characteristics:**

-   **Deliberate** (it requires conscious effort, voluntary activation)
-   **Slow** (seconds to minutes, laborious processing)
-   **Conscious** (you FEEL it working, a perceptible mental fatigue)
-   **Logical** (calculation, rational analysis, verification)
-   **Controlled** (you choose to activate it, you can turn it off)
-   **Serial processing** (one thing at a time, it cannot multitask)

**Examples of System 2 in action:**

-   Solving "17 × 24 = ?" (a mental calculation, an effort)
-   Filling out a tax return (attention, verification, tedious)
-   Parking in a tight spot for the first time (total concentration)
-   Comparing 2 complex products (features, pricing, reviews — analysis)
-   Monitoring your behavior in a formal social situation (self-control)
-   Checking the validity of a complex logical argument (a logic check)
-   Learning a new skill (copywriting, programming — a cognitive effort)

**System 2 is the brain's CEO** (it supervises, it decides on complex tasks).

**BUT: It is lazy** (it avoids work, it prefers to delegate to S1).

### **THE RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN THE SYSTEMS (The Problem):**

**System 1:**

-   It runs **ALWAYS** (24/7, on from the moment you wake up until you go to sleep)
-   It generates continuous **suggestions** (impressions, intuitions, feelings)
-   It's fast but **error-prone** (it makes systematic mistakes, it has biases)

**System 2:**

-   It **monitors occasionally** (most of the time it's asleep/lazy)
-   It **endorses or rejects** S1's suggestions (quality control)
-   It's slow but it **can correct** S1's errors (when it's activated)

**THE CRITICAL PROBLEM:**

**System 2 is LAZY** (it avoids effort).

**It accepts System 1's suggestions WITHOUT VERIFYING** (most of the time).

**The reason:** To conserve mental energy (glucose in the brain, a finite resource).

**The result:**

**Decisions are the product of automatic impressions (System 1)**

**NOT the product of careful analysis (System 2)**

**Most of the time:**

System 1 suggests → System 2 automatically approves (without checking).

### **A CLASSIC EXPERIMENT (The Bat and Ball Problem):**

Kahneman (_Thinking, Fast and Slow_, p. 44):

**The question:**

> "A bat and a ball cost $1.10 in total.
> 
> The bat costs $1.00 MORE than the ball.
> 
> How much does the ball cost?"

**System 1's answer** (it comes to mind instantly):

**"$0.10"** (the intuitive, automatic answer; it seems obvious)

**System 2's verification** (if it's activated, the analysis):

"Wait. Let's check.

If the ball = $0.10, and the bat costs $1.00 MORE than the ball:

The bat = $0.10 + $1.00 = $1.10

The total = $0.10 (the ball) + $1.10 (the bat) = $1.20

**BUT the problem says the total is $1.10** (not $1.20).

Therefore, $0.10 is WRONG."

**The correct answer** (via algebra or trial-and-error):

The ball = **$0.05**

The bat = $0.05 + $1.00 = $1.05

The total = $0.05 + $1.05 = $1.10 ✅

**Tested on:**

Harvard, MIT, and Princeton students (top universities, with high SAT scores).

**The results (shocking):**

**50%+ GOT IT WRONG** (they answered $0.10).

Including elite students (supposedly the "best thinkers").

**Why did they get it wrong?**

**System 1** generated an intuitive answer ($0.10 — a simple pattern matching, "it sounds right").

**System 2** did NOT verify it (or it verified it superficially and failed to override S1).

**They accepted S1's suggestion without analysis** (cognitive laziness).

Kahneman:

> _"The defining characteristic of System 2 is that its operations are effortful, and one of its main characteristics is laziness — a reluctance to invest more effort than is strictly necessary._
> 
> _System 2 is lazy. It takes the path of least resistance._
> 
> _Many people are overconfident, prone to place too much faith in their intuitions (System 1)._
> 
> _They apparently find cognitive effort at least mildly unpleasant and avoid it as much as possible."_

### **IMPLICATIONS FOR COPYWRITING:**

#### **IMPLICATION #1 — Copy Must ACTIVATE System 1 (Not Require System 2)**

**System 2 = fatigue → resistance → abandonment.**

Copy that requires S2 (deep analysis, jargon, complex comparisons):

The prospect thinks: "Too much work to understand. It's tiring. I'm leaving." (a bounce)

**System 1 = fast action → an emotional decision → conversion.**

Copy that activates S1 (simplicity, emotion, storytelling):

The prospect thinks: "I get it instantly. I feel something. I'll act." (a conversion)

**S1-optimized copy:**

✅ **Simple language** (an 8th-grade reading level — Bly, Doc 9)

✅ **Storytelling** (narratives are processed automatically by S1, without effort)

✅ **Emotion** (fear, desire, anger directly activate S1)

✅ **Visuals** (images, videos are processed in milliseconds)

✅ **Specific numbers** (8,347 — the Availability Heuristic, S1 trusts them)

✅ **Analogies** (complex → simple via a familiar comparison)

**S2-demanding copy:**

❌ **Technical jargon** (requires mental translation, a glossary, effort)

❌ **Complex tables** (multiple variables, difficult comparisons, analysis)

❌ **Dense statistics** (p-values, confidence intervals, methodology — a PhD is required)

❌ **Excessive choices** (10+ options — The Jam Study, paralysis)

❌ **Mental calculations** (a complex ROI, NPV, compound percentages)

**The Jam Study (Sheena Iyengar, Columbia, 2000):**

A supermarket. A jam tasting booth.

**Booth A:** 24 varieties (a massive choice)

**Booth B:** 6 varieties (a limited choice)

**The results:**

| Metric | 24 Varieties | 6 Varieties |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Traffic** (who stopped) | 60% | 40% |
| **Purchases** | 3% | 30% |

**24 varieties:**

-   More traffic (curiosity)
-   **BUT: 10x FEWER purchases** (paralysis — S2 is overloaded, it gives up)

**6 varieties:**

-   Less traffic
-   **10x MORE purchases** (S1 decides quickly, it's manageable)

**Excessive choice overloads System 2** (analysis paralysis).

**Limited choice = System 1 decides** (fast, emotional).

**In your copy:**

❌ "15 plans available (see the comparison table with 47 features)"

→ S2 overload → Abandonment

✅ "2 options:

• Basic: $497 (the core only)  
• Premium: $997 (the core + bonuses + support) — **78% choose this one**"

→ S1 processes it (2 are manageable), social proof (78%) helps the decision

✅✅ "1 ideal option for you: Premium $997"

→ Zero choice = zero paralysis (even easier)

#### **IMPLICATION #2 — Heuristics Are S1's Shortcuts (Explore Them, Don't Fight Them)**

System 1 uses **heuristics** (rules of thumb) to decide quickly.

**The next 6 sections (3.2-3.7):** Each heuristic is detailed.

**A preview:**

1.  **Availability:** Easy to remember = frequent (a vivid story > stats)
2.  **Representativeness:** A fit with a stereotype (specific personas)
3.  **Anchoring:** The first number = a reference (control the anchor)
4.  **Loss Aversion:** Losing hurts 2x+ more (frame it as "avoiding a loss")
5.  **Framing:** The presentation alters the decision (75% lean > 25% fat)
6.  **The Endowment Effect:** Owning = valuing it 2-3x more (trials, ownership language)

## **SECTION 3.2 — THE AVAILABILITY HEURISTIC**

### **THE PRINCIPLE:**

> "We judge the frequency or probability of events by the ease with which examples come to mind."

**An S1 shortcut:** "If I can remember it easily = it must be common/probable."

It works most of the time (common things ARE easier to remember).

**But:** It makes systematic errors when **vividness ≠ frequency** (rare but dramatic events are super-available, they seem more common than they are).

### **THE EXPERIMENTS:**

**Words with K:**

"Are there more words that BEGIN with K or that have K in the THIRD position?"

The intuitive answer: "Begin with K" (easy to remember: Kitchen, King, Know).

**The reality:** K in the 3rd position is **2x+ more common** (Ask, Like, Make, Take, etc.).

**Why the error:** We search for words by their first letter (a search strategy). Searching by the 3rd letter is difficult (a cognitive load). Therefore: Words that begin with K are easier to remember = they seem more common (the heuristic fails).

**Famous Couples:**

A list with 20 famous men (Brad Pitt, etc.) + 20 non-famous women.

"Were there more men or women on the list?"

The answer: "More men" (they are more memorable).

**The reality:** 50/50 (equal).

**The mechanism:** The famous men are more available (easier to remember) = they seem to be the majority.

**Divorce (Emotionality Increases Availability):**

Participants read **vivid stories** of tragic divorces (betrayal, financial ruin, custody battles — emotionally intense).

Then they estimate: "What is the probability of YOUR divorce in the next 5 years?"

**The result:** The estimate goes up by **+40%** (vs. a control group without the stories).

**Nothing changed in their marriages** (the same relationships, the same problems).

**But:** The vivid stories made "divorce" **super-available** mentally → It seems more probable (the heuristic is amplified by emotion).

**Causes of Death:**

"What kills more people annually: shark attacks or airplane crashes?"

The common answer: "Sharks" (or that they're tied).

**The reality:** Airplane crashes kill **30x+ more** (statistically).

**Why the error:**

Shark attacks are sensational (the media covers them intensely, "Shark Week," the movie Jaws) → They are **super-available** (it's easy to remember cases).

Airplane crashes are individually less vivid (an aggregated statistic, not specific memorable stories).

**Availability (sharks) ≠ Real frequency (airplanes).**

S1 uses availability (and is wrong). S2 could correct it (if it consulted the data), but it doesn't activate (it's lazy).
